### Tool result

_source line 11 · 2026-05-19T18:12:35.177Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LRPTq1ASJKFSnUc1mY8vXR`</summary>

```text
1	Recursive synthetic training, where each model generation fine-tunes on its predecessor's outputs, causes model collapse: a progressive loss of distributional diversity and downstream capability.
2	Detecting collapse currently requires costly text generation or benchmark evaluation at every generation.
3	Spectral analysis of internal representations offers a cheaper alternative, but the spectral dynamics of recursive collapse lack systematic characterization, and no prior work connects spectral indicators to closed-loop prevention.
4	We track spectral properties of the embedding Gram matrix across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M, with pilot validation on Pythia-1B.
5	Our analysis reveals a reproducible three-phase pattern: spectral decompression (generations 0--2), where effective rank and spectral log-volume (SLV) increase even as downstream performance degrades; collapse onset (generations 2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (generations 4--10), where spectral contraction proceeds at 2.43$\times$ the onset-generation rate.
6	Among the indicators we examine, SLV is particularly suited for automated monitoring: it requires only a forward pass on held-out data, captures distributed volume changes across all spectral directions, and exhibits post-decompression monotonic decline amenable to threshold-based control.
7	By varying the real-data mixing ratio across five levels, we construct a collapse phase diagram that localizes the critical mixing ratio $r_{\text{crit}}$ to the interval $(0.25, 0.3]$, separating monotonic spectral expansion from a transitional regime with initial contraction followed by recovery.
8	We further design a proof-of-concept proportional controller and discover a signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing prevents the spectral contraction that would activate the controller, so reactive spectral feedback becomes ineffective precisely when prevention succeeds.
9	
```

1	
2	Synthetic data has become integral to language model training.
3	Instruction tuning, knowledge distillation, and data augmentation all routinely use model-generated text, and as synthetic corpora are recycled into training pipelines, the process often becomes recursive: each generation of models is fine-tuned on outputs from the previous one.
4	Recent work demonstrates that such recursive synthetic training triggers \textit{model collapse}, a progressive erosion of distributional tails and downstream capability~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024tails, alemohammad2024mad}.
5	The problem is especially pressing as web-scraped corpora increasingly contain model-generated content, a trend that introduces implicit recursive loops even without deliberate data reuse.
6	
7	While model collapse is now well-characterized through theoretical analysis~\cite{dohmatob2024tails} and empirical studies~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024mad}, its effect on the geometric structure of learned representations remains poorly understood.
8	Characterizing how the embedding space contracts during recursive training could yield computationally cheap collapse indicators from internal model states alone, without text generation or benchmark evaluation.
9	
10	Current collapse detection depends on downstream metrics such as perplexity and benchmark accuracy, or on analysis of generated text through lexical diversity measures.
11	Both approaches require either costly inference or external evaluation infrastructure at every generation.
12	SIGMA~\cite{gu2026sigma} introduced spectral monitoring through embedding covariance analysis and showed that spectral indicators can track collapse, but validated only on OPT-125M over short generation horizons.
13	Four gaps remain open.
14	First, no work has systematically characterized spectral dynamics beyond a few generations at 410M+ parameters.
15	Second, the temporal relationship between spectral indicators and downstream degradation has not been examined.
16	Third, no study has connected spectral indicators to closed-loop collapse prevention through the lens of early warning signals~\cite{scheffer2009ews}.
17	Fourth, the relationship between real-data mixing ratios and spectral collapse has not been mapped: it is unknown how much real data suffices to prevent spectral contraction, or whether a sharp phase boundary exists.
18	
19	We address these gaps by tracking spectral indicators of the embedding Gram matrix across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M-deduped~\cite{biderman2023pythia}, with pilot validation on Pythia-1B-deduped.
20	Our analysis reveals a reproducible three-phase pattern (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}): spectral decompression (Gen~0--2), where effective rank ($r_{\text{eff}}$) and spectral log-volume (SLV) rise while downstream performance already declines; collapse onset (Gen~2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (Gen~4--10), where contraction proceeds at 2.43$\times$ the onset-generation rate.
21	Among these indicators, SLV is particularly suited for automated monitoring: it requires only a forward pass on held-out data (no text generation), captures distributed volume changes across all spectral directions, and declines monotonically after its peak.
22	We further design a proof-of-concept proportional controller and discover a signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing prevents the spectral contraction that would trigger the controller, so reactive spectral feedback becomes ineffective precisely when collapse prevention succeeds.
23	
24	Our contributions are:
25	\begin{itemize}
26	\item \textbf{C1:} The first joint spectral-downstream characterization at 410M+ scale: a three-phase collapse pattern (decompression, onset, 2.43$\times$ acceleration) across 10 generations (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}, Table~\ref{tab:phase0}).
27	\item \textbf{C2:} SLV as a computationally cheap collapse monitor with monotonic post-peak decline and a HellaSwag-spectral asymmetry showing downstream degradation precedes spectral collapse (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}).
28	\item \textbf{C3:} A signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing suppresses the spectral signal reactive controllers depend on, a structural limitation formalized via $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}, \S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
29	\item \textbf{C4:} A spectral collapse phase diagram that maps the boundary between collapse and stability across multiple mixing ratios, and enables empirical localization of $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}, \S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
30	\end{itemize}
31	
```

```text
1	\subsection{Spectral Metrics}
2	\label{subsec:spectral_metrics}
3	
4	Given a language model at generation $t$, we extract last-layer embeddings for $n{=}1{,}000$ held-out samples to form $\mathbf{E} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d}$ and compute the Gram matrix $\mathbf{G} = \mathbf{E}^\top \mathbf{E}$.
5	Let $\sigma_1 \geq \sigma_2 \geq \cdots \geq \sigma_d$ denote the singular values of $\mathbf{E}$.
6	We track three complementary spectral indicators:
7	
8	\paragraph{Spectral Log-Volume (SLV).}
9	$\text{SLV} = \sum_i \log \sigma_i = \frac{1}{2} \log \det \mathbf{G}$.
10	This quantity measures the total volume spanned by the embedding cloud in log-space.
11	Because it sums over all singular values, SLV is sensitive to contraction along any spectral direction, not only the dominant ones.
12	
13	\paragraph{Effective Rank.}
14	$r_{\text{eff}} = \exp(H(\mathbf{p}))$, where $p_i = \sigma_i / \sum_j \sigma_j$ and $H$ is the Shannon entropy, measuring how many dimensions carry substantial variance (uniform $\to d$, concentrated $\to 1$).
15	
16	\paragraph{Leading Singular Value.}
17	$\sigma_1$ tracks the dominant spectral direction; rising $\sigma_1$ under falling $r_{\text{eff}}$ signals representational collapse into a low-dimensional subspace.
18	
19	\subsection{Adaptive Mixing Controller}
20	\label{subsec:controller}
21	
22	We propose a closed-loop controller that adjusts the real-data mixing ratio $r_t$ at each generation based on the rate of spectral change.
23	The update rule is:
24	\begin{equation}
25	\label{eq:controller}
26	r_{t+1} = \frac{B_{\text{rem}}}{T - t} + k \cdot \max(0,\; \Delta_t - \tau)
27	\end{equation}
28	where $B_{\text{rem}}$ is the remaining real-data budget (fraction of total budget not yet consumed), $T$ is the total number of generations, $\tau{=}0.002$ is a sensitivity threshold, and $k{=}10$ is the controller gain.
29	The signal $\Delta_t$ is the baseline-normalized SLV drop: $\Delta_t = (\widetilde{S}_{t-1} - \widetilde{S}_t) / |\widetilde{S}_0|$, where $\widetilde{S}_t$ is the exponential moving average (EMA)-smoothed SLV ($\alpha{=}0.5$) and $\widetilde{S}_0$ is initialized from the pretrained model (Gen~0, SLV${=}2366.68$).
30	The signed form ensures that spectral decompression ($\Delta_t < 0$) does not trigger the controller; only contraction activates the $\max(0, \cdot)$ gate in Eq.~\ref{eq:controller}.
31	The output is clipped to $[r_{\min}, r_{\max}] = [0.20, 0.80]$ and subject to the global constraint $\sum_t r_t \leq B_{\text{total}}$.
32	
33	The first term distributes the remaining budget uniformly across future generations; the model degrades gracefully even without spectral signal.
34	The second term injects additional real data when SLV contraction exceeds the threshold $\tau$, proportional to the severity of the spectral drop.
35	This design requires no lookahead and operates with a single hyperparameter choice ($k$) beyond the budget constraint.
36	
37	\paragraph{Controller Silence Condition.}
38	Define $r_{\text{crit}}$ as the minimum constant mixing ratio such that SLV is non-decreasing from the second generation onward: $\widetilde{S}_t \geq \widetilde{S}_{t-1}$ for all $t \in \{2, \ldots, T\}$ (excluding the initial fine-tuning transient at $t{=}1$).
39	When a constant ratio $r \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ is applied, $\Delta_t \leq 0 < \tau$ for all $t \geq 2$ and any $\tau > 0$, so the controller boost is identically zero.
40	This $\tau$-independence means the paradox cannot be resolved by lowering the sensitivity threshold; the same argument extends to cumulative variants.
41	Our experiments (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}) empirically bound $0.25 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.3$ over Gen~2--10 for Pythia-410M on OpenWebText.
42	
43	\subsection{Second-Order Spectral Indicator}
44	\label{subsec:second_order}
45	
46	The signal attenuation paradox (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) arises because the first-order signal $\Delta_t$ becomes non-positive under sufficient real-data mixing.
47	We define a second-order indicator based on the rate of change of the SLV growth:
48	\begin{equation}
49	\label{eq:second_order}
50	\Delta^2_t = \widetilde{\Delta}_{t-1} - \widetilde{\Delta}_t
51	\end{equation}
52	where $\widetilde{\Delta}_t$ is the EMA-smoothed first-order signal with smoothing parameter $\alpha_2$ (a tunable hyperparameter; we use $\alpha_2{=}0.3$ in our experiments).
53	Even when SLV rises monotonically ($\Delta_t < 0$ for all $t \geq 2$), a sustained negative $\Delta^2_t$ suggests that the growth rate is generally decelerating, a potential precursor to eventual stagnation or reversal.
54	Whether this deceleration signal generalizes across mixing ratios remains an open question (\S\ref{sec:conclusion}).
55	
56	\subsection{Collapse Phase Diagram}
57	\label{subsec:phase_diagram}
58	
59	We define the \emph{collapse phase diagram} as the mapping from constant mixing ratio $r$ to the SLV trajectory over $T$ generations.
60	For each $r$, we classify the outcome: \emph{collapse} if SLV declines monotonically after the decompression phase (Gen~2 onward), \emph{stable} if SLV is non-decreasing, and \emph{transitional} if SLV shows non-monotonic behavior after Gen~2 (both increases and decreases within the observation window).
61	The critical mixing ratio $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) is the boundary between collapse and stability in this diagram.
62	We estimate $r_{\text{crit}}$ by evaluating multiple constant ratios and identifying the lowest $r$ for which $\widetilde{S}_t \geq \widetilde{S}_{t-1}$ holds for all $t \in \{2, \ldots, T\}$.
63	
```

```text
1	
2	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
3	\label{subsec:setup}
4	
5	We use Pythia-410M-deduped \cite{biderman2023pythia} as our base model and fine-tune on a 50K-subset of OpenWebText.
6	Starting from the pretrained checkpoint (generation~0), each subsequent generation fine-tunes on synthetic text produced by the previous generation, for a total of 10 recursive generations.
7	At each generation we compute the spectral indicators from \S\ref{subsec:spectral_metrics} on the Gram matrix of 1K held-out last-layer embeddings, paired with three downstream metrics: perplexity, HellaSwag accuracy (0-shot), and distinct-4.
8	
9	For the mixing comparison (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}), we evaluate three strategies sharing the same total real-data budget:
10	(1)~\textbf{pure collapse} ($r_t{=}0$),
11	(2)~\textbf{fixed mix} ($r_t{=}0.50$), and
12	(3)~\textbf{adaptive} ($k{=}10$, Eq.~\ref{eq:controller}).
13	
14	\subsection{Spectral Collapse Characterization}
15	\label{subsec:phase0}
16	
17	Table~\ref{tab:phase0} reports spectral and downstream metrics across 10 recursive generations, which reveal a three-phase structure.
18	
19	\begin{table}[t]
20	\centering
21	\small
22	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrrrc@{}}
23	\toprule
24	Gen & $r_{\text{eff}}$ & SLV & PPL & HeSw & D-4 & $\sigma_1$ \\
25	\midrule
26	0  & 417.55 & 2366.68 &  9.55 & .413 & .741 & 1942.8 \\
27	2  & \textbf{496.44} & \textbf{2469.93} & 13.58 & .376 & .515 &  712.0 \\
28	5  & 479.21 & 2370.17 & 24.59 & .344 & .463 &  741.0 \\
29	7  & 464.20 & 2304.19 & 38.89 & .319 & .447 &  791.3 \\
30	10 & 442.46 & 2178.95 & 76.78 & .295 & .426 &  825.7 \\
31	\bottomrule
32	\end{tabular}
33	\caption{Spectral and downstream metrics at selected generations (Pythia-410M; full trajectory described in text). Bold marks the spectral peak before collapse onset.}
34	\label{tab:phase0}
35	\end{table}
36	
37	\begin{figure}[t]
38	\centering
39	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/spectral_trajectory.pdf}
40	\caption{SLV across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M under pure-synthetic training (solid) and 50\% real-data mixing (dashed). Three phases are visible in the pure-synthetic trajectory: decompression (Gen~0--2, SLV peaks at 2470), onset (Gen~2--4), and acceleration (Gen~4--10, SLV falls 12\% from peak to 2179). Under 50\% mixing, SLV rises monotonically (except a negligible Gen~1 dip) to 2572 at Gen~10, 8.7\% above baseline and 102 points above the pure-collapse peak.}
41	\label{fig:spectral_trajectory}
42	\end{figure}
43	
44	\paragraph{Phase I: Spectral Decompression (Gen 0--2).}
45	We identify phase boundaries at inflection points where $r_{\text{eff}}$ and SLV transition from rising to declining.
46	In the first two generations, $r_{\text{eff}}$ rises from 417.55 to 496.44 (+18.9\%) and SLV increases from 2366.68 to 2469.93 (+4.4\%), while $\sigma_1$ drops sharply from 1942.8 to 712.0 as spectral mass redistributes from the dominant direction to smaller components.
47	Crucially, this decompression does not indicate improved model quality: HellaSwag drops from 0.413 to 0.376 ($-$8.9\%) and distinct-4 falls from 0.741 to 0.515 ($-$30.5\%) during the same interval.
48	HellaSwag already declines 5.6\% from Gen~0 to Gen~1, precisely when spectral indicators are still rising; this asymmetry suggests that decompression captures distributional broadening rather than preservation of task-supporting representations.
49	A monitoring system relying solely on spectral metrics during Phase~I would incorrectly conclude the model remains healthy; joint spectral-downstream tracking~\cite{gu2026sigma} is essential to detect early collapse.
50	The value of SLV lies not in temporal precedence over downstream metrics, but in requiring no text generation or external benchmark evaluation; it supports continuous monitoring at training time.
51	
52	\paragraph{Phase II: Collapse Onset (Gen 2--4).}
53	Both $r_{\text{eff}}$ and SLV reverse after Gen~2 ($r_{\text{eff}}$: 496.44$\to$493.31, SLV: 2470$\to$2441), and this reversal marks the transition from decompression to collapse while perplexity continues its monotonic rise.
54	
55	\paragraph{Phase III: Accelerating Collapse (Gen 4--10).}
56	Spectral contraction accelerates: the mean per-generation percentage $r_{\text{eff}}$ decline in Phase~III is 2.43$\times$ the onset-generation rate (Gen~2$\to$3).
57	By Gen~10, $r_{\text{eff}}$ falls to 442.46 ($-$10.9\% from peak), SLV drops to 2178.95 ($-$7.9\% from baseline), perplexity reaches 76.78 ($+$704\%), and $\sigma_1$ reverses its earlier decline (700.7$\to$825.7), a sign of renewed spectral concentration (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}).
58	
59	\subsection{Cross-Scale Validation}
60	\label{subsec:cross_scale}
61	
62	Repeating the protocol on Pythia-1B (Gen~0--3) confirms the same decompression-onset structure ($r_{\text{eff}}$ peak at Gen~2, SLV peak one generation earlier). SLV's low cost and monotonic post-peak decline make it well-suited as an automated collapse monitor.
63	
64	\subsection{Adaptive Mixing Comparison}
65	\label{subsec:phase1}
66	
67	Under pure synthetic training (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}), SLV peaks at Gen~2 (2470) and declines monotonically thereafter.
68	With 50\% real-data mixing, SLV rises over nine of ten generations, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 (102 points above the pure-collapse peak); a negligible dip at Gen~1 ($\Delta_1 \approx 0.0003$, well below $\tau$) reflects the initial fine-tuning transient.
69	
70	The adaptive controller ($k{=}10$) remains silent through all ten generations: $\Delta_t$ is non-positive from Gen~2 onward, and the sole positive value ($\Delta_1 \approx 0.0003$) falls $6\times$ below $\tau{=}0.002$.
71	Since $\Delta_t \leq 0$ for all $t \geq 2$, the controller stays silent for any finite $k$ and any $\tau > 0$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}); larger gain values do not escape the paradox.
72	Since pure collapse ($r{=}0$) produces SLV decline from Gen~3 while fixed mix ($r{=}0.5$) yields sustained growth from Gen~2, and $r{=}0.2$ falls below $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}), we bound $0.2 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.5$.
73	
74	Despite sustained spectral expansion, downstream metrics degrade steadily: perplexity increases 58\% ($9.55 \to 15.10$) and distinct-4 falls 68\% ($0.741 \to 0.239$) over ten generations.
75	This reinforces the asymmetry from \S\ref{subsec:phase0}: spectral health under mixed training does not guarantee model health, and the signal attenuation paradox renders per-generation reactive controllers ineffective precisely when collapse prevention succeeds.
76	
77	\subsection{Alternative EWS Comparison}
78	\label{subsec:alt_ews}
79	
80	To assess whether the spectral patterns in \S\ref{subsec:phase0} reflect a broader embedding-level geometric phenomenon rather than an artifact of a single metric, we compare SLV against six alternative collapse indicators from three signal sources: embedding geometry (RankMe, isotropy), attention weights (stable rank, spectral norm), and output distributions (token entropy, perplexity growth rate).
81	Token entropy is the Shannon entropy of the argmax logit distribution over a fixed evaluation set and requires no text generation.
82	
83	Table~\ref{tab:ews_comparison} summarizes the comparison under pure-synthetic training ($r{=}0$).
84	Two indicators share SLV's decompression-then-collapse trajectory: RankMe~\cite{rankme2023}, the exponential of the normalized singular-value entropy, peaks at Gen~2 and declines monotonically thereafter, though with 2.5$\times$ lower signal-to-noise ratio (4.7\% relative change vs.\ 11.8\% for SLV).
85	Weight-level and output-level indicators (stable rank, spectral norm, token entropy) change monotonically from Gen~0 with no decompression phase; they cannot distinguish collapse onset from the initial fine-tuning transient.
86	Isotropy is the least reliable indicator: random sampling without a fixed seed introduces approximately 5\% variance, and it exhibits six monotonicity violations across ten generations, with direction reversals at Gen~4+ that produce a misleading recovery signal.
87	
88	\begin{table}[t]
89	\centering
90	\small
91	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
92	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcrrc@{}}
93	\toprule
94	Indicator & Source & Peak & Mono? & Rel $\Delta$ & Rev.? \\
95	\midrule
96	SLV         & emb-Gram  & Gen 2 & \checkmark & 11.8\% & \checkmark \\
97	RankMe      & emb-SVD   & Gen 2 & \checkmark &  4.7\% & \checkmark \\
98	Token ent.  & logit     & ---   & \checkmark &  9.6\% & \checkmark \\
99	Stable rank & attn wt.  & ---   & \checkmark & 14.0\% & \checkmark \\
100	Spec.\ norm & attn wt.  & ---   & \checkmark &  8.0\% & \checkmark \\
101	Isotropy    & emb       & ---   & $\times$\,(6) & 75\%   & \checkmark \\
102	PPL growth  & ppl       & ---   & $\sim$\,(1) &  ---   & \checkmark \\
103	\bottomrule
104	\end{tabular}
105	\caption{Alternative early warning indicators under pure-synthetic training ($r{=}0$, Pythia-410M, 10 gen). \textbf{Peak}: generation at which the indicator reverses (--- = monotonic from Gen~0). \textbf{Mono?}: post-peak monotonicity (\checkmark\ = yes; number = violations). \textbf{Rel $\Delta$}: total relative change Gen~0--10. \textbf{Rev.?}: direction reversal under $r{=}0.5$ mixing (\checkmark\ = reversed).}
106	\label{tab:ews_comparison}
107	\end{table}
108	
109	The final column of Table~\ref{tab:ews_comparison} reveals that all seven indicators reverse direction under 50\% real-data mixing.
110	This universality confirms that the signal attenuation paradox (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) is not specific to SLV: any per-generation reactive controller based on these indicators will fall silent when mixing exceeds $r_{\text{crit}}$.
111	Among embedding-level metrics, SLV offers the highest signal-to-noise ratio (2.5$\times$ that of RankMe) and, together with RankMe, is the only indicator that exhibits a decompression baseline to distinguish collapse onset from training transients.
112	
113	\subsection{Collapse Phase Diagram}
114	\label{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}
115	
116	We now map SLV trajectories across mixing ratios to construct the collapse phase diagram defined in \S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}.
117	Three qualitatively distinct trajectory types emerge:
118	
119	\paragraph{Type~I: Pure Collapse ($r{=}0$).}
120	SLV exhibits large decompression ($+$98 points at Gen~1, peaking at Gen~2) followed by accelerating contraction (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}).
121	This trajectory defines the baseline collapse regime.
122	
123	\paragraph{Type~II: Near-critical Mixing ($r{=}0.2$).}
124	Twenty percent real data suppresses the SLV decompression phase: SLV rises only 0.8 points from Gen~0 to Gen~1 (vs.\ $+$98 under $r{=}0$), dips at Gen~2 ($2367.5 \to 2358.3$, $-9.2$), then recovers through Gen~3--5 ($2362.5 \to 2379.2 \to 2399.0$, accelerating at $+4.2$, $+16.7$, $+19.7$ per generation), surpassing the Gen~0 baseline by 32.2 points at Gen~5.
125	Because $\widetilde{S}_2 < \widetilde{S}_1$, the non-decreasing condition is violated and $r{=}0.2$ formally lies below $r_{\text{crit}}$.
126	The trajectory is consistent with the transitional regime (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}): initial contraction followed by recovery and expansion.
127	Even at $r{=}0.2$, where SLV dips at Gen~2, the smoothed signal never exceeds the controller threshold ($\max \Delta_t = 0.0019 < \tau = 0.002$), so as little as 20\% real data silences the controller entirely.
128	The effective rank still rises at Gen~2 ($476.7 \to 481.6$) while the EMA-smoothed SLV registers a net decline ($\widetilde{S}_2 < \widetilde{S}_1$), evidence that smoothed SLV detects spectral contraction earlier at this mixing ratio.
129	
130	At $r{=}0.25$, the full 10-generation trajectory follows the same Type~II pattern: SLV dips from Gen~0 to Gen~1 ($2366.7 \to 2362.1$, $-4.6$), reaches a minimum at Gen~2 ($2363.1$), then rises monotonically through Gen~10 ($2485.0$, $+5.0\%$ above baseline).
131	The EMA-smoothed sequence confirms the sub-critical classification: $\widetilde{S}_1 = 2364.4$, $\widetilde{S}_2 = 2363.8$, satisfying $\widetilde{S}_2 < \widetilde{S}_1$ and violating the non-decreasing condition.
132	Controller silence is more pronounced than at $r{=}0.2$: $\max \Delta_t = 0.00028 < \tau = 0.002$, a $6.7\times$ weaker signal than the $r{=}0.2$ maximum ($0.0019$), consistent with further signal attenuation as the real-data fraction increases.
133	
134	\paragraph{Type~III: Super-critical Mixing ($r{=}0.5$).}
135	SLV rises monotonically from Gen~2 onward, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 ($+$8.7\% above baseline), consistent with $r{=}0.5 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
136	
137	These three types partition the mixing-ratio axis into collapse ($r < r_{\text{crit}}$), transitional, and stable ($r \geq r_{\text{crit}}$) zones.
138	At $r{=}0.3$, SLV expands monotonically from Gen~2 to 2511.1 at Gen~10 ($+$6.1\% above baseline), with sustained positive growth across all eight post-transient generations.
139	This confirms $r{=}0.3 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ over the full 10-generation horizon, tightening the bound to $0.25 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.3$.
140	
141	The largest single-generation SLV drop in the first two generations shrinks with $r$: $-9.2$ at $r{=}0.2$ (Gen~1$\to$2), $-4.6$ at $r{=}0.25$ (Gen~0$\to$1), and $-0.6$ at $r{=}0.3$ (Gen~0$\to$1), consistent with a continuous crossover rather than a sharp boundary.
142	The Type~II trajectory clarifies what $r_{\text{crit}}$ marks in practice: not a boundary between collapse and stability in final outcome, but a transition in trajectory shape.
143	Below $r_{\text{crit}}$, real-data mixing fails to suppress the initial SLV contraction transient but sustains spectral expansion over the observed five generations once the transient passes.
144	
```

```text
1	
2	We presented a systematic spectral characterization of model collapse across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M and identified a three-phase trajectory (decompression, onset, 2.43$\times$ acceleration) confirmed at the 1B scale.
3	SLV is particularly suited for automated monitoring: it requires only a forward pass, declines monotonically after its peak, and captures volume changes before they manifest as dimensionality reduction.
4	By varying the mixing ratio across five levels, we construct a collapse phase diagram that localizes $r_{\text{crit}} \in (0.25, 0.3]$: above this threshold SLV expands monotonically (Type~III), while just below it a transitional regime (Type~II) exhibits an initial contraction transient followed by recovery.
5	A proof-of-concept proportional controller exposes a signal attenuation paradox.
6	The maximum per-generation controller signal weakens monotonically with the real-data fraction: $\max \Delta_t = 0.0019$ at $r{=}0.2$ (just below $\tau{=}0.002$), $0.00028$ at $r{=}0.25$ ($6.7\times$ weaker), and no decline signal at $r \geq 0.3$ where SLV expands monotonically.
7	The controller falls silent along a continuous gradient, not at a single threshold.
8	Because all seven EWS indicators reverse under sufficient mixing (\S\ref{subsec:alt_ews}), this paradox generalizes beyond SLV: any per-generation reactive controller faces the same tradeoff between prevention and detectability~\cite{scheffer2009ews}.
9	Even when mixing prevents spectral collapse, monitoring retains value: $r_{\text{crit}}$ varies across domains and architectures, and spectral indicators reveal whether the current ratio lies in the transitional or stable zone without waiting for downstream degradation.
10	
11	The signal attenuation paradox motivates three directions for future work.
12	First, cumulative indicators that integrate spectral trajectory shape across generations may detect sub-critical regimes where per-generation differences vanish.
13	Second, non-spectral auxiliary signals (perplexity acceleration, diversity collapse rate) could provide complementary triggers that do not attenuate with mixing.
14	Third, hybrid approaches combining spectral EWS with periodic probing at reduced real-data fractions may sustain controller sensitivity without sacrificing collapse prevention.
15	Validation at larger scales and longer horizons remains essential.
16	
```

```text
1	
2	\paragraph{Model Collapse.}
3	\citet{shumailov2024collapse} showed that recursive training on model-generated data progressively erodes the tails of learned distributions, a phenomenon they termed \emph{model collapse}.
4	\citet{dohmatob2024tails} formalized this decay through scaling laws that predict tail erosion as a function of recursive depth, and \citet{dohmatob2024demystified} provided a regression-theoretic analysis of the underlying mechanism.
5	The self-consuming loop accelerates collapse even under data accumulation~\cite{alemohammad2024mad}, and \citet{dohmatob2025strong} demonstrated that as little as 1\% synthetic contamination suffices to trigger degradation, though \citet{gerstgrasser2024inevitable} showed that preserving all prior real data can delay it.
6	These studies diagnose collapse through output distributions, benchmark scores, or theoretical scaling laws.
7	We complement them by tracking collapse through the spectral geometry of internal representations, which requires no text generation or external evaluation.
8	
9	\paragraph{Spectral Analysis of Representations.}
10	Random matrix theory (RMT) applied to neural network weights reveals implicit regularization structure in the eigenspectrum~\cite{martin2021implicit}.
11	Effective rank~\cite{roy2007effectiverank} and its normalized variant RankMe~\cite{rankme2023} have become standard tools for quantifying representational dimensionality.
12	Recent work has connected spectral properties to training pathologies: \citet{he2025spectral} linked spectral collapse in hidden representations to plasticity loss in continual learning, and \citet{naderi2025mindgap} identified spectral rank collapse in attention matrices as a failure mode of deep transformers.
13	Most directly relevant, \citet{gu2026sigma} proposed SIGMA, a spectral monitoring framework that tracks singular-value distributions during recursive training on OPT-125M over short horizons.
14	We extend this line of work along several axes: 10-generation recursive tracking at 410M and 1B scale, a three-phase temporal characterization of spectral evolution, a mixing-ratio phase diagram that maps the boundary between spectral collapse and stability, and a connection between spectral indicators and closed-loop prevention.
15	
16	\paragraph{Early Warning Signals and Critical Transitions.}
17	The theory of early warning signals (EWS) for critical transitions originates in ecology, where \citet{scheffer2009ews} established that rising autocorrelation and variance precede regime shifts in complex systems.
18	\citet{dakos2012methods} developed a methodological toolbox for detecting these signals, and \citet{bury2021deeplearning} showed that deep learning can identify EWS in systems where classical indicators fail.
19	A central tension in EWS theory is that stabilization interventions suppress the very precursors they rely on~\cite{scheffer2009ews}.
20	We encounter a parallel tension in model collapse, but the failure mode is distinct: ecological stabilization reduces the signal-to-noise ratio while preserving signal direction, whereas real-data mixing reverses the spectral trajectory and eliminates the signal entirely (\S\ref{subsec:controller}).
21	\citet{he2025spectral} provides a bridge between these domains by connecting spectral collapse to critical transitions in continual learning.
22	
23	\paragraph{Data Mixing for LLM Training.}
24	DoReMi~\cite{xie2023doremi} optimizes domain weights through a small proxy model, and Data Mixing Laws~\cite{ye2025mixinglaws, data_mixing_laws2024} derive predictive formulas for optimal composition from small-scale experiments.
25	Aioli~\cite{chen2025aioli} unifies several online mixing strategies under a single framework and adjusts domain weights during training.
26	All three approaches rely on loss-based objectives.
27	Our controller differs in two respects: it uses a spectral signal (SLV, the log-volume of embedding Gram matrices) rather than loss, and it targets a specific pathology (recursive collapse) rather than general domain balance.
28	
```

paper/sections/experiments.tex:72:Since pure collapse ($r{=}0$) produces SLV decline from Gen~3 while fixed mix ($r{=}0.5$) yields sustained growth from Gen~2, and $r{=}0.2$ falls below $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}), we bound $0.2 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.5$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:110:This universality confirms that the signal attenuation paradox (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) is not specific to SLV: any per-generation reactive controller based on these indicators will fall silent when mixing exceeds $r_{\text{crit}}$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:125:Because $\widetilde{S}_2 < \widetilde{S}_1$, the non-decreasing condition is violated and $r{=}0.2$ formally lies below $r_{\text{crit}}$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:135:SLV rises monotonically from Gen~2 onward, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 ($+$8.7\% above baseline), consistent with $r{=}0.5 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:137:These three types partition the mixing-ratio axis into collapse ($r < r_{\text{crit}}$), transitional, and stable ($r \geq r_{\text{crit}}$) zones.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:139:This confirms $r{=}0.3 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ over the full 10-generation horizon, tightening the bound to $0.25 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.3$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:142:The Type~II trajectory clarifies what $r_{\text{crit}}$ marks in practice: not a boundary between collapse and stability in final outcome, but a transition in trajectory shape.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:143:Below $r_{\text{crit}}$, real-data mixing fails to suppress the initial SLV contraction transient but sustains spectral expansion over the observed five generations once the transient passes.
paper/sections/conclusion.tex:4:By varying the mixing ratio across five levels, we construct a collapse phase diagram that localizes $r_{\text{crit}} \in (0.25, 0.3]$: above this threshold SLV expands monotonically (Type~III), while just below it a transitional regime (Type~II) exhibits an initial contraction transient followed by recovery.
paper/sections/conclusion.tex:9:Even when mixing prevents spectral collapse, monitoring retains value: $r_{\text{crit}}$ varies across domains and architectures, and spectral indicators reveal whether the current ratio lies in the transitional or stable zone without waiting for downstream degradation.
paper/sections/method.tex:38:Define $r_{\text{crit}}$ as the minimum constant mixing ratio such that SLV is non-decreasing from the second generation onward: $\widetilde{S}_t \geq \widetilde{S}_{t-1}$ for all $t \in \{2, \ldots, T\}$ (excluding the initial fine-tuning transient at $t{=}1$).
paper/sections/method.tex:39:When a constant ratio $r \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ is applied, $\Delta_t \leq 0 < \tau$ for all $t \geq 2$ and any $\tau > 0$, so the controller boost is identically zero.
paper/sections/method.tex:41:Our experiments (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}) empirically bound $0.25 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.3$ over Gen~2--10 for Pythia-410M on OpenWebText.
paper/sections/method.tex:61:The critical mixing ratio $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) is the boundary between collapse and stability in this diagram.
paper/sections/method.tex:62:We estimate $r_{\text{crit}}$ by evaluating multiple constant ratios and identifying the lowest $r$ for which $\widetilde{S}_t \geq \widetilde{S}_{t-1}$ holds for all $t \in \{2, \ldots, T\}$.
paper/sections/abstract.tex:7:By varying the real-data mixing ratio across five levels, we construct a collapse phase diagram that localizes the critical mixing ratio $r_{\text{crit}}$ to the interval $(0.25, 0.3]$, separating monotonic spectral expansion from a transitional regime with initial contraction followed by recovery.
paper/sections/introduction.tex:28:\item \textbf{C3:} A signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing suppresses the spectral signal reactive controllers depend on, a structural limitation formalized via $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}, \S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
paper/sections/introduction.tex:29:\item \textbf{C4:} A spectral collapse phase diagram that maps the boundary between collapse and stability across multiple mixing ratios, and enables empirical localization of $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}, \S\ref{subsec:phase1}).

paradox (\\S\\ref{subsec:controller}) is not specific to SLV: any per-generation reactive controller based on these indicators will fall silent when mixing exceeds ```text
paper/sections/experiments.tex:24:Gen & $r_{\text{eff}}$ & SLV & PPL & HeSw & D-4 & $\sigma_1$ \\
paper/sections/experiments.tex:45:We identify phase boundaries at inflection points where $r_{\text{eff}}$ and SLV transition from rising to declining.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:46:In the first two generations, $r_{\text{eff}}$ rises from 417.55 to 496.44 (+18.9\%) and SLV increases from 2366.68 to 2469.93 (+4.4\%), while $\sigma_1$ drops sharply from 1942.8 to 712.0 as spectral mass redistributes from the dominant direction to smaller components.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:53:Both $r_{\text{eff}}$ and SLV reverse after Gen~2 ($r_{\text{eff}}$: 496.44$\to$493.31, SLV: 2470$\to$2441), and this reversal marks the transition from decompression to collapse while perplexity continues its monotonic rise.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:56:Spectral contraction accelerates: the mean per-generation percentage $r_{\text{eff}}$ decline in Phase~III is 2.43$\times$ the onset-generation rate (Gen~2$\to$3).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:57:By Gen~10, $r_{\text{eff}}$ falls to 442.46 ($-$10.9\% from peak), SLV drops to 2178.95 ($-$7.9\% from baseline), perplexity reaches 76.78 ($+$704\%), and $\sigma_1$ reverses its earlier decline (700.7$\to$825.7), a sign of renewed spectral concentration (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:62:Repeating the protocol on Pythia-1B (Gen~0--3) confirms the same decompression-onset structure ($r_{\text{eff}}$ peak at Gen~2, SLV peak one generation earlier). SLV's low cost and monotonic post-peak decline make it well-suited as an automated collapse monitor.
paper/sections/method.tex:14:$r_{\text{eff}} = \exp(H(\mathbf{p}))$, where $p_i = \sigma_i / \sum_j \sigma_j$ and $H$ is the Shannon entropy, measuring how many dimensions carry substantial variance (uniform $\to d$, concentrated $\to 1$).
paper/sections/method.tex:17:$\sigma_1$ tracks the dominant spectral direction; rising $\sigma_1$ under falling $r_{\text{eff}}$ signals representational collapse into a low-dimensional subspace.
paper/sections/introduction.tex:20:Our analysis reveals a reproducible three-phase pattern (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}): spectral decompression (Gen~0--2), where effective rank ($r_{\text{eff}}$) and spectral log-volume (SLV) rise while downstream performance already declines; collapse onset (Gen~2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (Gen~4--10), where contraction proceeds at 2.43$\times$ the onset-generation rate.
```

```text
paper/sections/experiments.tex:70:The adaptive controller ($k{=}10$) remains silent through all ten generations: $\Delta_t$ is non-positive from Gen~2 onward, and the sole positive value ($\Delta_1 \approx 0.0003$) falls $6\times$ below $\tau{=}0.002$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:71:Since $\Delta_t \leq 0$ for all $t \geq 2$, the controller stays silent for any finite $k$ and any $\tau > 0$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}); larger gain values do not escape the paradox.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:127:Even at $r{=}0.2$, where SLV dips at Gen~2, the smoothed signal never exceeds the controller threshold ($\max \Delta_t = 0.0019 < \tau = 0.002$), so as little as 20\% real data silences the controller entirely.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:132:Controller silence is more pronounced than at $r{=}0.2$: $\max \Delta_t = 0.00028 < \tau = 0.002$, a $6.7\times$ weaker signal than the $r{=}0.2$ maximum ($0.0019$), consistent with further signal attenuation as the real-data fraction increases.
paper/sections/conclusion.tex:6:The maximum per-generation controller signal weakens monotonically with the real-data fraction: $\max \Delta_t = 0.0019$ at $r{=}0.2$ (just below $\tau{=}0.002$), $0.00028$ at $r{=}0.25$ ($6.7\times$ weaker), and no decline signal at $r \geq 0.3$ where SLV expands monotonically.
paper/sections/method.tex:26:r_{t+1} = \frac{B_{\text{rem}}}{T - t} + k \cdot \max(0,\; \Delta_t - \tau)
paper/sections/method.tex:29:The signal $\Delta_t$ is the baseline-normalized SLV drop: $\Delta_t = (\widetilde{S}_{t-1} - \widetilde{S}_t) / |\widetilde{S}_0|$, where $\widetilde{S}_t$ is the exponential moving average (EMA)-smoothed SLV ($\alpha{=}0.5$) and $\widetilde{S}_0$ is initialized from the pretrained model (Gen~0, SLV${=}2366.68$).
paper/sections/method.tex:30:The signed form ensures that spectral decompression ($\Delta_t < 0$) does not trigger the controller; only contraction activates the $\max(0, \cdot)$ gate in Eq.~\ref{eq:controller}.
paper/sections/method.tex:39:When a constant ratio $r \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ is applied, $\Delta_t \leq 0 < \tau$ for all $t \geq 2$ and any $\tau > 0$, so the controller boost is identically zero.
paper/sections/method.tex:46:The signal attenuation paradox (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) arises because the first-order signal $\Delta_t$ becomes non-positive under sufficient real-data mixing.
paper/sections/method.tex:50:\Delta^2_t = \widetilde{\Delta}_{t-1} - \widetilde{\Delta}_t
paper/sections/method.tex:53:Even when SLV rises monotonically ($\Delta_t < 0$ for all $t \geq 2$), a sustained negative $\Delta^2_t$ suggests that the growth rate is generally decelerating, a potential precursor to eventual stagnation or reversal.
```

paper/sections/experiments.tex:72:Since pure collapse ($r{=}0$) produces SLV decline from Gen~3 while fixed mix ($r{=}0.5$) yields sustained growth from Gen~2, and $r{=}0.2$ falls below $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}), we bound $0.2 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.5$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:83:Table~\ref{tab:ews_comparison} summarizes the comparison under pure-synthetic training ($r{=}0$).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:105:\caption{Alternative early warning indicators under pure-synthetic training ($r{=}0$, Pythia-410M, 10 gen). \textbf{Peak}: generation at which the indicator reverses (--- = monotonic from Gen~0). \textbf{Mono?}: post-peak monotonicity (\checkmark\ = yes; number = violations). \textbf{Rel $\Delta$}: total relative change Gen~0--10. \textbf{Rev.?}: direction reversal under $r{=}0.5$ mixing (\checkmark\ = reversed).}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:119:\paragraph{Type~I: Pure Collapse ($r{=}0$).}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:123:\paragraph{Type~II: Near-critical Mixing ($r{=}0.2$).}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:124:Twenty percent real data suppresses the SLV decompression phase: SLV rises only 0.8 points from Gen~0 to Gen~1 (vs.\ $+$98 under $r{=}0$), dips at Gen~2 ($2367.5 \to 2358.3$, $-9.2$), then recovers through Gen~3--5 ($2362.5 \to 2379.2 \to 2399.0$, accelerating at $+4.2$, $+16.7$, $+19.7$ per generation), surpassing the Gen~0 baseline by 32.2 points at Gen~5.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:125:Because $\widetilde{S}_2 < \widetilde{S}_1$, the non-decreasing condition is violated and $r{=}0.2$ formally lies below $r_{\text{crit}}$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:127:Even at $r{=}0.2$, where SLV dips at Gen~2, the smoothed signal never exceeds the controller threshold ($\max \Delta_t = 0.0019 < \tau = 0.002$), so as little as 20\% real data silences the controller entirely.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:130:At $r{=}0.25$, the full 10-generation trajectory follows the same Type~II pattern: SLV dips from Gen~0 to Gen~1 ($2366.7 \to 2362.1$, $-4.6$), reaches a minimum at Gen~2 ($2363.1$), then rises monotonically through Gen~10 ($2485.0$, $+5.0\%$ above baseline).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:132:Controller silence is more pronounced than at $r{=}0.2$: $\max \Delta_t = 0.00028 < \tau = 0.002$, a $6.7\times$ weaker signal than the $r{=}0.2$ maximum ($0.0019$), consistent with further signal attenuation as the real-data fraction increases.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:134:\paragraph{Type~III: Super-critical Mixing ($r{=}0.5$).}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:135:SLV rises monotonically from Gen~2 onward, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 ($+$8.7\% above baseline), consistent with $r{=}0.5 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:138:At $r{=}0.3$, SLV expands monotonically from Gen~2 to 2511.1 at Gen~10 ($+$6.1\% above baseline), with sustained positive growth across all eight post-transient generations.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:139:This confirms $r{=}0.3 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ over the full 10-generation horizon, tightening the bound to $0.25 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.3$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:141:The largest single-generation SLV drop in the first two generations shrinks with $r$: $-9.2$ at $r{=}0.2$ (Gen~1$\to$2), $-4.6$ at $r{=}0.25$ (Gen~0$\to$1), and $-0.6$ at $r{=}0.3$ (Gen~0$\to$1), consistent with a continuous crossover rather than a sharp boundary.
paper/sections/conclusion.tex:6:The maximum per-generation controller signal weakens monotonically with the real-data fraction: $\max \Delta_t = 0.0019$ at $r{=}0.2$ (just below $\tau{=}0.002$), $0.00028$ at $r{=}0.25$ ($6.7\times$ weaker), and no decline signal at $r \geq 0.3$ where SLV expands monotonically.

paper/sections/introduction.tex:20:Our analysis reveals a reproducible three-phase pattern (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}): spectral decompression (Gen~0--2), where effective rank ($r_{\text{eff}}$) and spectral log-volume (SLV) rise while downstream performance already declines; collapse onset (Gen~2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (Gen~4--10), where contraction proceeds at 2.43$\times$ the onset-generation rate.
paper/sections/introduction.tex:26:\item \textbf{C1:} The first joint spectral-downstream characterization at 410M+ scale: a three-phase collapse pattern (decompression, onset, 2.43$\times$ acceleration) across 10 generations (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}, Table~\ref{tab:phase0}).

```text
paper/sections/related_work.tex:3:\citet{shumailov2024collapse} showed that recursive training on model-generated data progressively erodes the tails of learned distributions, a phenomenon they termed \emph{model collapse}.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:4:\citet{dohmatob2024tails} formalized this decay through scaling laws that predict tail erosion as a function of recursive depth, and \citet{dohmatob2024demystified} provided a regression-theoretic analysis of the underlying mechanism.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:5:The self-consuming loop accelerates collapse even under data accumulation~\cite{alemohammad2024mad}, and \citet{dohmatob2025strong} demonstrated that as little as 1\% synthetic contamination suffices to trigger degradation, though \citet{gerstgrasser2024inevitable} showed that preserving all prior real data can delay it.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:10:Random matrix theory (RMT) applied to neural network weights reveals implicit regularization structure in the eigenspectrum~\cite{martin2021implicit}.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:11:Effective rank~\cite{roy2007effectiverank} and its normalized variant RankMe~\cite{rankme2023} have become standard tools for quantifying representational dimensionality.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:12:Recent work has connected spectral properties to training pathologies: \citet{he2025spectral} linked spectral collapse in hidden representations to plasticity loss in continual learning, and \citet{naderi2025mindgap} identified spectral rank collapse in attention matrices as a failure mode of deep transformers.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:13:Most directly relevant, \citet{gu2026sigma} proposed SIGMA, a spectral monitoring framework that tracks singular-value distributions during recursive training on OPT-125M over short horizons.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:17:The theory of early warning signals (EWS) for critical transitions originates in ecology, where \citet{scheffer2009ews} established that rising autocorrelation and variance precede regime shifts in complex systems.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:18:\citet{dakos2012methods} developed a methodological toolbox for detecting these signals, and \citet{bury2021deeplearning} showed that deep learning can identify EWS in systems where classical indicators fail.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:19:A central tension in EWS theory is that stabilization interventions suppress the very precursors they rely on~\cite{scheffer2009ews}.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:21:\citet{he2025spectral} provides a bridge between these domains by connecting spectral collapse to critical transitions in continual learning.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:24:DoReMi~\cite{xie2023doremi} optimizes domain weights through a small proxy model, and Data Mixing Laws~\cite{ye2025mixinglaws, data_mixing_laws2024} derive predictive formulas for optimal composition from small-scale experiments.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:25:Aioli~\cite{chen2025aioli} unifies several online mixing strategies under a single framework and adjusts domain weights during training.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:5:We use Pythia-410M-deduped \cite{biderman2023pythia} as our base model and fine-tune on a 50K-subset of OpenWebText.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:49:A monitoring system relying solely on spectral metrics during Phase~I would incorrectly conclude the model remains healthy; joint spectral-downstream tracking~\cite{gu2026sigma} is essential to detect early collapse.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:84:Two indicators share SLV's decompression-then-collapse trajectory: RankMe~\cite{rankme2023}, the exponential of the normalized singular-value entropy, peaks at Gen~2 and declines monotonically thereafter, though with 2.5$\times$ lower signal-to-noise ratio (4.7\% relative change vs.\ 11.8\% for SLV).
paper/sections/conclusion.tex:8:Because all seven EWS indicators reverse under sufficient mixing (\S\ref{subsec:alt_ews}), this paradox generalizes beyond SLV: any per-generation reactive controller faces the same tradeoff between prevention and detectability~\cite{scheffer2009ews}.
paper/sections/introduction.tex:4:Recent work demonstrates that such recursive synthetic training triggers \textit{model collapse}, a progressive erosion of distributional tails and downstream capability~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024tails, alemohammad2024mad}.
paper/sections/introduction.tex:7:While model collapse is now well-characterized through theoretical analysis~\cite{dohmatob2024tails} and empirical studies~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024mad}, its effect on the geometric structure of learned representations remains poorly understood.
paper/sections/introduction.tex:12:SIGMA~\cite{gu2026sigma} introduced spectral monitoring through embedding covariance analysis and showed that spectral indicators can track collapse, but validated only on OPT-125M over short generation horizons.
paper/sections/introduction.tex:16:Third, no study has connected spectral indicators to closed-loop collapse prevention through the lens of early warning signals~\cite{scheffer2009ews}.
paper/sections/introduction.tex:19:We address these gaps by tracking spectral indicators of the embedding Gram matrix across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M-deduped~\cite{biderman2023pythia}, with pilot validation on Pythia-1B-deduped.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FSkC53xpG5TMsrqkCmVma3`</summary>

```text
paper/sections/experiments.tex:24:Gen & $r_{\text{eff}}$ & SLV & PPL & HeSw & D-4 & $\sigma_1$ \\
paper/sections/experiments.tex:40:\caption{SLV across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M under pure-synthetic training (solid) and 50\% real-data mixing (dashed). Three phases are visible in the pure-synthetic trajectory: decompression (Gen~0--2, SLV peaks at 2470), onset (Gen~2--4), and acceleration (Gen~4--10, SLV falls 12\% from peak to 2179). Under 50\% mixing, SLV rises monotonically (except a negligible Gen~1 dip) to 2572 at Gen~10, 8.7\% above baseline and 102 points above the pure-collapse peak.}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:44:\paragraph{Phase I: Spectral Decompression (Gen 0--2).}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:48:HellaSwag already declines 5.6\% from Gen~0 to Gen~1, precisely when spectral indicators are still rising; this asymmetry suggests that decompression captures distributional broadening rather than preservation of task-supporting representations.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:52:\paragraph{Phase II: Collapse Onset (Gen 2--4).}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:53:Both $r_{\text{eff}}$ and SLV reverse after Gen~2 ($r_{\text{eff}}$: 496.44$\to$493.31, SLV: 2470$\to$2441), and this reversal marks the transition from decompression to collapse while perplexity continues its monotonic rise.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:55:\paragraph{Phase III: Accelerating Collapse (Gen 4--10).}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:56:Spectral contraction accelerates: the mean per-generation percentage $r_{\text{eff}}$ decline in Phase~III is 2.43$\times$ the onset-generation rate (Gen~2$\to$3).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:57:By Gen~10, $r_{\text{eff}}$ falls to 442.46 ($-$10.9\% from peak), SLV drops to 2178.95 ($-$7.9\% from baseline), perplexity reaches 76.78 ($+$704\%), and $\sigma_1$ reverses its earlier decline (700.7$\to$825.7), a sign of renewed spectral concentration (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:62:Repeating the protocol on Pythia-1B (Gen~0--3) confirms the same decompression-onset structure ($r_{\text{eff}}$ peak at Gen~2, SLV peak one generation earlier). SLV's low cost and monotonic post-peak decline make it well-suited as an automated collapse monitor.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:67:Under pure synthetic training (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}), SLV peaks at Gen~2 (2470) and declines monotonically thereafter.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:68:With 50\% real-data mixing, SLV rises over nine of ten generations, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 (102 points above the pure-collapse peak); a negligible dip at Gen~1 ($\Delta_1 \approx 0.0003$, well below $\tau$) reflects the initial fine-tuning transient.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:70:The adaptive controller ($k{=}10$) remains silent through all ten generations: $\Delta_t$ is non-positive from Gen~2 onward, and the sole positive value ($\Delta_1 \approx 0.0003$) falls $6\times$ below $\tau{=}0.002$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:72:Since pure collapse ($r{=}0$) produces SLV decline from Gen~3 while fixed mix ($r{=}0.5$) yields sustained growth from Gen~2, and $r{=}0.2$ falls below $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}), we bound $0.2 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.5$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:84:Two indicators share SLV's decompression-then-collapse trajectory: RankMe~\cite{rankme2023}, the exponential of the normalized singular-value entropy, peaks at Gen~2 and declines monotonically thereafter, though with 2.5$\times$ lower signal-to-noise ratio (4.7\% relative change vs.\ 11.8\% for SLV).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:85:Weight-level and output-level indicators (stable rank, spectral norm, token entropy) change monotonically from Gen~0 with no decompression phase; they cannot distinguish collapse onset from the initial fine-tuning transient.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:86:Isotropy is the least reliable indicator: random sampling without a fixed seed introduces approximately 5\% variance, and it exhibits six monotonicity violations across ten generations, with direction reversals at Gen~4+ that produce a misleading recovery signal.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:96:SLV         & emb-Gram  & Gen 2 & \checkmark & 11.8\% & \checkmark \\
paper/sections/experiments.tex:97:RankMe      & emb-SVD   & Gen 2 & \checkmark &  4.7\% & \checkmark \\
paper/sections/experiments.tex:105:\caption{Alternative early warning indicators under pure-synthetic training ($r{=}0$, Pythia-410M, 10 gen). \textbf{Peak}: generation at which the indicator reverses (--- = monotonic from Gen~0). \textbf{Mono?}: post-peak monotonicity (\checkmark\ = yes; number = violations). \textbf{Rel $\Delta$}: total relative change Gen~0--10. \textbf{Rev.?}: direction reversal under $r{=}0.5$ mixing (\checkmark\ = reversed).}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:120:SLV exhibits large decompression ($+$98 points at Gen~1, peaking at Gen~2) followed by accelerating contraction (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:124:Twenty percent real data suppresses the SLV decompression phase: SLV rises only 0.8 points from Gen~0 to Gen~1 (vs.\ $+$98 under $r{=}0$), dips at Gen~2 ($2367.5 \to 2358.3$, $-9.2$), then recovers through Gen~3--5 ($2362.5 \to 2379.2 \to 2399.0$, accelerating at $+4.2$, $+16.7$, $+19.7$ per generation), surpassing the Gen~0 baseline by 32.2 points at Gen~5.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:127:Even at $r{=}0.2$, where SLV dips at Gen~2, the smoothed signal never exceeds the controller threshold ($\max \Delta_t = 0.0019 < \tau = 0.002$), so as little as 20\% real data silences the controller entirely.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:128:The effective rank still rises at Gen~2 ($476.7 \to 481.6$) while the EMA-smoothed SLV registers a net decline ($\widetilde{S}_2 < \widetilde{S}_1$), evidence that smoothed SLV detects spectral contraction earlier at this mixing ratio.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:130:At $r{=}0.25$, the full 10-generation trajectory follows the same Type~II pattern: SLV dips from Gen~0 to Gen~1 ($2366.7 \to 2362.1$, $-4.6$), reaches a minimum at Gen~2 ($2363.1$), then rises monotonically through Gen~10 ($2485.0$, $+5.0\%$ above baseline).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:135:SLV rises monotonically from Gen~2 onward, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 ($+$8.7\% above baseline), consistent with $r{=}0.5 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:138:At $r{=}0.3$, SLV expands monotonically from Gen~2 to 2511.1 at Gen~10 ($+$6.1\% above baseline), with sustained positive growth across all eight post-transient generations.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:141:The largest single-generation SLV drop in the first two generations shrinks with $r$: $-9.2$ at $r{=}0.2$ (Gen~1$\to$2), $-4.6$ at $r{=}0.25$ (Gen~0$\to$1), and $-0.6$ at $r{=}0.3$ (Gen~0$\to$1), consistent with a continuous crossover rather than a sharp boundary.
paper/sections/method.tex:29:The signal $\Delta_t$ is the baseline-normalized SLV drop: $\Delta_t = (\widetilde{S}_{t-1} - \widetilde{S}_t) / |\widetilde{S}_0|$, where $\widetilde{S}_t$ is the exponential moving average (EMA)-smoothed SLV ($\alpha{=}0.5$) and $\widetilde{S}_0$ is initialized from the pretrained model (Gen~0, SLV${=}2366.68$).
paper/sections/method.tex:41:Our experiments (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}) empirically bound $0.25 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.3$ over Gen~2--10 for Pythia-410M on OpenWebText.
paper/sections/method.tex:60:For each $r$, we classify the outcome: \emph{collapse} if SLV declines monotonically after the decompression phase (Gen~2 onward), \emph{stable} if SLV is non-decreasing, and \emph{transitional} if SLV shows non-monotonic behavior after Gen~2 (both increases and decreases within the observation window).
paper/sections/introduction.tex:20:Our analysis reveals a reproducible three-phase pattern (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}): spectral decompression (Gen~0--2), where effective rank ($r_{\text{eff}}$) and spectral log-volume (SLV) rise while downstream performance already declines; collapse onset (Gen~2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (Gen~4--10), where contraction proceeds at 2.43$\times$ the onset-generation rate.
paper/sections/limitations.tex:5:We validate at two model scales only (Pythia-410M and a 1B pilot through Gen~3), leaving open whether the three-phase pattern holds at larger scales or with different architectures.
```

paper/sections/related_work.tex:5:The self-consuming loop accelerates collapse even under data accumulation~\cite{alemohammad2024mad}, and \citet{dohmatob2025strong} demonstrated that as little as 1\% synthetic contamination suffices to trigger degradation, though \citet{gerstgrasser2024inevitable} showed that preserving all prior real data can delay it.

Three phases are visible in the pure-synthetic trajectory: decompression (Gen~0--2, SLV peaks at 2470), onset (Gen~2--4), and acceleration (Gen~4--10, SLV falls 12\% from peak to 2179). Under 50\% mixing, SLV rises monotonically (except a negligible Gen~1 dip) to 2572 at Gen~10, 8.7\% above baseline and 102 points above the pure-collapse peak.}
paper/sections/experiments.tex:46:In the first two generations, $r_{\text{eff}}$ rises from 417.55 to 496.44 (+18.9\%) and SLV increases from 2366.68 to 2469.93 (+4.4\%), while $\sigma_1$ drops sharply from 1942.8 to 712.0 as spectral mass redistributes from the dominant direction to smaller components.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:47:Crucially, this decompression does not indicate improved model quality: HellaSwag drops from 0.413 to 0.376 ($-$8.9\%) and distinct-4 falls from 0.741 to 0.515 ($-$30.5\%) during the same interval.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:48:HellaSwag already declines 5.6\% from Gen~0 to Gen~1, precisely when spectral indicators are still rising; this asymmetry suggests that decompression captures distributional broadening rather than preservation of task-supporting representations.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:57:By Gen~10, $r_{\text{eff}}$ falls to 442.46 ($-$10.9\% from peak), SLV drops to 2178.95 ($-$7.9\% from baseline), perplexity reaches 76.78 ($+$704\%), and $\sigma_1$ reverses its earlier decline (700.7$\to$825.7), a sign of renewed spectral concentration (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:68:With 50\% real-data mixing, SLV rises over nine of ten generations, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 (102 points above the pure-collapse peak); a negligible dip at Gen~1 ($\Delta_1 \approx 0.0003$, well below $\tau$) reflects the initial fine-tuning transient.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:74:Despite sustained spectral expansion, downstream metrics degrade steadily: perplexity increases 58\% ($9.55 \to 15.10$) and distinct-4 falls 68\% ($0.741 \to 0.239$) over ten generations.

paper/sections/experiments.tex:86:Isotropy is the least reliable indicator: random sampling without a fixed seed introduces approximately 5\% variance, and it exhibits six monotonicity violations across ten generations, with direction reversals at Gen~4+ that produce a misleading recovery signal.

5:Our analysis reveals a reproducible three-phase pattern: spectral decompression (generations 0--2), where effective rank and spectral log-volume (SLV) increase even as downstream performance degrades; collapse onset (generations 2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (generations 4--10), where spectral contraction proceeds at 2.43$\times$ the onset-generation rate.
6:Among the indicators we examine, SLV is particularly suited for automated monitoring: it requires only a forward pass on held-out data, captures distributed volume changes across all spectral directions, and exhibits post-decompression monotonic decline amenable to threshold-based control.
```

paper/sections/related_work.tex:17:The theory of early warning signals (EWS) for critical transitions originates in ecology, where \citet{scheffer2009ews} established that rising autocorrelation and variance precede regime shifts in complex systems.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:18:\citet{dakos2012methods} developed a methodological toolbox for detecting these signals, and \citet{bury2021deeplearning} showed that deep learning can identify EWS in systems where classical indicators fail.
paper/sections/related_work.tex:19:A central tension in EWS theory is that stabilization interventions suppress the very precursors they rely on~\cite{scheffer2009ews}.

paper/sections/conclusion.tex:8:Because all seven EWS indicators reverse under sufficient mixing (\S\ref{subsec:alt_ews}), this paradox generalizes beyond SLV: any per-generation reactive controller faces the same tradeoff between prevention and detectability~\cite{scheffer2009ews}.
paper/sections/conclusion.tex:14:Third, hybrid approaches combining spectral EWS with periodic probing at reduced real-data fractions may sustain controller sensitivity without sacrificing collapse prevention.

```text
paper/sections/related_work.tex:20:We encounter a parallel tension in model collapse, but the failure mode is distinct: ecological stabilization reduces the signal-to-noise ratio while preserving signal direction, whereas real-data mixing reverses the spectral trajectory and eliminates the signal entirely (\S\ref{subsec:controller}).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:7:At each generation we compute the spectral indicators from \S\ref{subsec:spectral_metrics} on the Gram matrix of 1K held-out last-layer embeddings, paired with three downstream metrics: perplexity, HellaSwag accuracy (0-shot), and distinct-4.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:9:For the mixing comparison (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}), we evaluate three strategies sharing the same total real-data budget:
paper/sections/experiments.tex:67:Under pure synthetic training (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}), SLV peaks at Gen~2 (2470) and declines monotonically thereafter.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:71:Since $\Delta_t \leq 0$ for all $t \geq 2$, the controller stays silent for any finite $k$ and any $\tau > 0$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}); larger gain values do not escape the paradox.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:72:Since pure collapse ($r{=}0$) produces SLV decline from Gen~3 while fixed mix ($r{=}0.5$) yields sustained growth from Gen~2, and $r{=}0.2$ falls below $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}), we bound $0.2 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.5$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:75:This reinforces the asymmetry from \S\ref{subsec:phase0}: spectral health under mixed training does not guarantee model health, and the signal attenuation paradox renders per-generation reactive controllers ineffective precisely when collapse prevention succeeds.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:80:To assess whether the spectral patterns in \S\ref{subsec:phase0} reflect a broader embedding-level geometric phenomenon rather than an artifact of a single metric, we compare SLV against six alternative collapse indicators from three signal sources: embedding geometry (RankMe, isotropy), attention weights (stable rank, spectral norm), and output distributions (token entropy, perplexity growth rate).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:110:This universality confirms that the signal attenuation paradox (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) is not specific to SLV: any per-generation reactive controller based on these indicators will fall silent when mixing exceeds $r_{\text{crit}}$.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:116:We now map SLV trajectories across mixing ratios to construct the collapse phase diagram defined in \S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:120:SLV exhibits large decompression ($+$98 points at Gen~1, peaking at Gen~2) followed by accelerating contraction (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}).
paper/sections/experiments.tex:126:The trajectory is consistent with the transitional regime (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}): initial contraction followed by recovery and expansion.
paper/sections/experiments.tex:135:SLV rises monotonically from Gen~2 onward, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 ($+$8.7\% above baseline), consistent with $r{=}0.5 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
paper/sections/conclusion.tex:8:Because all seven EWS indicators reverse under sufficient mixing (\S\ref{subsec:alt_ews}), this paradox generalizes beyond SLV: any per-generation reactive controller faces the same tradeoff between prevention and detectability~\cite{scheffer2009ews}.
paper/sections/method.tex:41:Our experiments (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}) empirically bound $0.25 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.3$ over Gen~2--10 for Pythia-410M on OpenWebText.
paper/sections/method.tex:46:The signal attenuation paradox (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) arises because the first-order signal $\Delta_t$ becomes non-positive under sufficient real-data mixing.
paper/sections/method.tex:61:The critical mixing ratio $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) is the boundary between collapse and stability in this diagram.
paper/sections/introduction.tex:26:\item \textbf{C1:} The first joint spectral-downstream characterization at 410M+ scale: a three-phase collapse pattern (decompression, onset, 2.43$\times$ acceleration) across 10 generations (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}, Table~\ref{tab:phase0}).
paper/sections/introduction.tex:27:\item \textbf{C2:} SLV as a computationally cheap collapse monitor with monotonic post-peak decline and a HellaSwag-spectral asymmetry showing downstream degradation precedes spectral collapse (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}).
paper/sections/introduction.tex:28:\item \textbf{C3:} A signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing suppresses the spectral signal reactive controllers depend on, a structural limitation formalized via $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}, \S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
paper/sections/introduction.tex:29:\item \textbf{C4:} A spectral collapse phase diagram that maps the boundary between collapse and stability across multiple mixing ratios, and enables empirical localization of $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}, \S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
```

    "originalFile": "\nSynthetic data has become integral to language model training.\nInstruction tuning, knowledge distillation, and data augmentation all routinely use model-generated text, and as synthetic corpora are recycled into training pipelines, the process often becomes recursive: each generation of models is fine-tuned on outputs from the previous one.\nRecent work demonstrates that such recursive synthetic training triggers \\textit{model collapse}, a progressive erosion of distributional tails and downstream capability~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024tails, alemohammad2024mad}.\nThe problem is especially pressing as web-scraped corpora increasingly contain model-generated content, a trend that introduces implicit recursive loops even without deliberate data reuse.\n\nWhile model collapse is now well-characterized through theoretical analysis~\\cite{dohmatob2024tails} and empirical studies~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024mad}, its effect on the geometric structure of learned representations remains poorly understood.\nCharacterizing how the embedding space contracts during recursive training could yield computationally cheap collapse indicators from internal model states alone, without text generation or benchmark evaluation.\n\nCurrent collapse detection depends on downstream metrics such as perplexity and benchmark accuracy, or on analysis of generated text through lexical diversity measures.\nBoth approaches require either costly inference or external evaluation infrastructure at every generation.\nSIGMA~\\cite{gu2026sigma} introduced spectral monitoring through embedding covariance analysis and showed that spectral indicators can track collapse, but validated only on OPT-125M over short generation horizons.\nFour gaps remain open.\nFirst, no work has systematically characterized spectral dynamics beyond a few generations at 410M+ parameters.\nSecond, the temporal relationship between spectral indicators and downstream degradation has not been examined.\nThird, no study has connected spectral indicators to closed-loop collapse prevention through the lens of early warning signals~\\cite{scheffer2009ews}.\nFourth, the relationship between real-data mixing ratios and spectral collapse has not been mapped: it is unknown how much real data suffices to prevent spectral contraction, or whether a sharp phase boundary exists.\n\nWe address these gaps by tracking spectral indicators of the embedding Gram matrix across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M-deduped~\\cite{biderman2023pythia}, with pilot validation on Pythia-1B-deduped.\nOur analysis reveals a reproducible three-phase pattern (Figure~\\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}): spectral decompression (Gen~0--2), where effective rank ($r_{\\text{eff}}$) and spectral log-volume (SLV) rise while downstream performance already declines; collapse onset (Gen~2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (Gen~4--10), where contraction proceeds at 2.43$\\times$ the onset-generation rate.\nAmong these indicators, SLV is particularly suited for automated monitoring: it requires only a forward pass on held-out data (no text generation), captures distributed volume changes across all spectral directions, and declines monotonically after its peak.\nWe further design a proof-of-concept proportional controller and discover a signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing prevents the spectral contraction that would trigger the controller, so reactive spectral feedback becomes ineffective precisely when collapse prevention succeeds.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{C1:} The first joint spectral-downstream characterization at 410M+ scale: a three-phase collapse pattern (decompression, onset, 2.43$\\times$ acceleration) across 10 generations (\\S\\ref{subsec:phase0}, Table~\\ref{tab:phase0}).\n\\item \\textbf{C2:} SLV as a computationally cheap collapse monitor with monotonic post-peak decline and a HellaSwag-spectral asymmetry showing downstream degradation precedes spectral collapse (\\S\\ref{subsec:phase0}).\n\\item \\textbf{C3:} A signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing suppresses the spectral signal reactive controllers depend on, a structural limitation formalized via $r_{\\text{crit}}$ (\\S\\ref{subsec:controller}, \\S\\ref{subsec:phase1}).\n\\item \\textbf{C4:} A spectral collapse phase diagram that maps the boundary between collapse and stability across multiple mixing ratios, and enables empirical localization of $r_{\\text{crit}}$ (\\S\\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}, \\S\\ref{subsec:phase1}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "originalFile": "\n\\paragraph{Model Collapse.}\n\\citet{shumailov2024collapse} showed that recursive training on model-generated data progressively erodes the tails of learned distributions, a phenomenon they termed \\emph{model collapse}.\n\\citet{dohmatob2024tails} formalized this decay through scaling laws that predict tail erosion as a function of recursive depth, and \\citet{dohmatob2024demystified} provided a regression-theoretic analysis of the underlying mechanism.\nThe self-consuming loop accelerates collapse even under data accumulation~\\cite{alemohammad2024mad}, and \\citet{dohmatob2025strong} demonstrated that as little as 1\\% synthetic contamination suffices to trigger degradation, though \\citet{gerstgrasser2024inevitable} showed that preserving all prior real data can delay it.\nThese studies diagnose collapse through output distributions, benchmark scores, or theoretical scaling laws.\nWe complement them by tracking collapse through the spectral geometry of internal representations, which requires no text generation or external evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Spectral Analysis of Representations.}\nRandom matrix theory (RMT) applied to neural network weights reveals implicit regularization structure in the eigenspectrum~\\cite{martin2021implicit}.\nEffective rank~\\cite{roy2007effectiverank} and its normalized variant RankMe~\\cite{rankme2023} have become standard tools for quantifying representational dimensionality.\nRecent work has connected spectral properties to training pathologies: \\citet{he2025spectral} linked spectral collapse in hidden representations to plasticity loss in continual learning, and \\citet{naderi2025mindgap} identified spectral rank collapse in attention matrices as a failure mode of deep transformers.\nMost directly relevant, \\citet{gu2026sigma} proposed SIGMA, a spectral monitoring framework that tracks singular-value distributions during recursive training on OPT-125M over short horizons.\nWe extend this line of work along several axes: 10-generation recursive tracking at 410M and 1B scale, a three-phase temporal characterization of spectral evolution, a mixing-ratio phase diagram that maps the boundary between spectral collapse and stability, and a connection between spectral indicators and closed-loop prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Early Warning Signals and Critical Transitions.}\nThe theory of early warning signals (EWS) for critical transitions originates in ecology, where \\citet{scheffer2009ews} established that rising autocorrelation and variance precede regime shifts in complex systems.\n\\citet{dakos2012methods} developed a methodological toolbox for detecting these signals, and \\citet{bury2021deeplearning} showed that deep learning can identify EWS in systems where classical indicators fail.\nA central tension in EWS theory is that stabilization interventions suppress the very precursors they rely on~\\cite{scheffer2009ews}.\nWe encounter a parallel tension in model collapse, but the failure mode is distinct: ecological stabilization reduces the signal-to-noise ratio while preserving signal direction, whereas real-data mixing reverses the spectral trajectory and eliminates the signal entirely (\\S\\ref{subsec:controller}).\n\\citet{he2025spectral} provides a bridge between these domains by connecting spectral collapse to critical transitions in continual learning.\n\n\\paragraph{Data Mixing for LLM Training.}\nDoReMi~\\cite{xie2023doremi} optimizes domain weights through a small proxy model, and Data Mixing Laws~\\cite{ye2025mixinglaws, data_mixing_laws2024} derive predictive formulas for optimal composition from small-scale experiments.\nAioli~\\cite{chen2025aioli} unifies several online mixing strategies under a single framework and adjusts domain weights during training.\nAll three approaches rely on loss-based objectives.\nOur controller differs in two respects: it uses a spectral signal (SLV, the log-volume of embedding Gram matrices) rather than loss, and it targets a specific pathology (recursive collapse) rather than general domain balance.\n",
